    {
      "id": "https://www.capetown.travel/travel-like-a-local-your-neighbourhood-guide-to-green-point/",
      "title": "Your Neighbourhood Guide to Green Point",
      "url": "https://www.capetown.travel/travel-like-a-local-your-neighbourhood-guide-to-green-point/",
      "text": "# Your Neighbourhood Guide to Green Point\n\n- Cape Town Tourism\n- October 14, 2025\n- Arts & Culture, Eat & Drink, Information, Shopping\n- 5 min Read\n- Click to listen to this article\n\nGreen Point is one of Cape Town’s more popular neighbourhoods, it buzzes with activity, is lined with Instaworthy restaurants, scenic walking and cycling trails, and has plenty of art galleries to visit. . \n\nHere’s everything you can enjoy in Green Point and surrounding areas on your next visit: \n\n## Things To Do   \n\n### **DHL Stadium   **\n\nThe DHL Stadium, also known as Cape Town Stadium, is the go-to destination for the city’s biggest events and sporting action. From epic rugby clashes to world-class concerts, this iconic venue has become a true landmark in the Mother City. Keep an eye on their website and social channels for upcoming events. \n\nLocation: Fritz Sonnenberg Rd, Green Point \nWebsite: dhlstadium.co.za/  \n\n### **Green Point Urban Park **\n\nA unique space in the heart of an urban environment, the Green Point Urban Park is a fantastic, family-friendly place for a picnic, walk, run, or simply lazing around. Be sure to visit the new 3D dome, part of the City of Cape Town’s Experiential Education Garden (EEG). This new development celebrates heritage and biodiversity. It also features an outdoor gym, multiple children’s play areas, an adventure park, and even a tranquil labyrinth. Access to the park is via Bill Peters Drive, Bay Road, or Helen Suzman Boulevard. \n\nPrice: Free \nWebsite: bit.ly/4h1JNGH  \n\n### **The Blue Train Park **\n\nThe Blue Train Park with its miniature blue train captures the youthful fantasies of both young and old. Operated by the Sea Point Rotary Club and around for 60 years, this fun train trip offers some incredible views of the Atlantic Ocean, Lion’s Head, and Table Mountain. \n\nPrice: R45 pp, which includes entrance + 1 train ride \nWebsite:  www.thebluetrainpark.co.za \n\n### Putt-Putt \n\nLooking for something  affordable and adventurous? Swing by for a game of putt-putt at Putt Putt Golf Courses. Grab your little ones, plan a cute date, or go out with your mates, you’re bound to enjoy hours of fun, complete with epic Cape Town views. \n\nLocation: Beach Road, Three Anchor Bay \nWebsite: www.puttputtcapetown.co.za \n\n### Cycling with CURIOCITY Green Point\n\nGreen Point is one of the best spots in Cape Town for a scenic and easygoing bike ride. Just minutes from the V&A Waterfront and lows into the Sea Point Promenade, it’s the perfect base for two-wheeled adventures. CURIOCITY Green Point offers bike rentals for two-hour sessions, making it super easy to explore the neighbourhood, cruise down to the beach, or roll along the prom and stop for a coffee or ice cream with ocean views. Rentals can be arranged at the front desk. \n\nLocation: CURIOCITY Green Point at 153 Main Rd, Green Point \nWebsite: curiocity.africa/  \n\n### Art Gallery Hopping\n\nAdd a splash of creativity to your day with a visit to one of Green Point’s art galleries. The area features a growing collection of contemporary and independent galleries showcasing work by both emerging and established South African artists. Galleries to add to your list include the Goodman Gallery, Palette Fine Art Gallery, and Creation Gallery.\n\n### Cape Quarter Lifestyle Village\n\nPerfectly positioned between Green Point and De Waterkant, Cape Quarter Lifestyle Village is a sleek urban escape that blends boutique shopping, curated local design, cosy cafés, and wellness studios – all under one stylish roof.\n\nWhether you’re browsing for unique finds, sipping a flat white, or unwinding at a yoga class, this vibrant lifestyle hub brings together the best of Cape Town’s creativity, culture, and charm.\n\nLocation: 27 Somerset Rd, Green Point\nWebsite: capequarter.co.za\n\n## Where To Eat\n\n### Hudsons\n\nIf you’re craving a seriously good burger, stop by Hudsons – The Burger Joint. With over 18 burgers on offer, including plant-based choices, it’s a top pick for burger enthusiasts.\n\nLocation: Shop 1A, Portside Mall, Main Rd, Green Point\nWebsite: www.theburgerjoint.co.za\n\n### Gold Restaurant\n\nExperience an African culinary journey at Gold Restaurant, where you can enjoy authentic cuisine from across the continent, including a 14-dish tasting safari.\n\nLocation: 15 Bennett St, Green Point\nWebsite: www.goldrestaurant.co.za\n\n### Giovanni’s Deliworld\n\nGiovanni’s is a local favourite known for its Italian flair. The deli offers both cosy seating for those who want to dine in, and a convenient takeaway option – perfect for a picnic or a day out in Cape Town. Inside, you’ll find everything from authentic Italian pantry staples to artisanal baked goods and a tempting selection of gourmet meals.\n\nLocation: 103 Main Rd, Green Point\nWebsite: giovannisdeliworld.co.za\n\n### El Burro\n\nEl Burro is considered one of the best Mexican restaurants in Cape Town. Think Mexican tapas-style dining, with everything from tacos to enchiladas, all handmade with care. Pair it with one of their legendary margaritas and you’ve got a recipe for a seriously good time.\n\nLocation: 79 Main Rd, Greenpoint,\nWebsite: www.elburro.co.za\n\n### Pigalle\n\nPigalle, located in the heart of Green Point, is among the city’s largest indoor restaurant venues. This fine-dining restaurant is open for dinner and private events, serving classic dishes with a Portuguese twist. Note the dress code: no caps or shorts allowed.\n\nThe restaurant recently won three awards at the  World Luxury Restaurant Awards 2025, including Best International Cuisine  (Africa), Best Luxury Entertainment Restaurant (Southern Africa), and Best Luxury Seafood Restaurant (South Africa).\n\nLocation: 57A Somerset Rd, Green Point\nWebsite: www.pigalle.capetown\n\n## Where To Stay\n\nWhether you’re visiting for a weekend escape or an extended stay, Green Point offers accommodation options for every type of traveller.\n\n### ANEW Hotel Greenpoint\n\nWith panoramic views of Signal Hill and the Atlantic, ANEW Hotel Greenpoint is an eco-conscious base just steps from the area’s top spots. Conveniently located just off Green Point Main Road, the hotel is also within walking distance of many local landmarks and the V&A Waterfront. Amenities include free parking and Wi-Fi, access to the rooftop pool and fitness center, 24-hour check-in service, and complimentary breakfast.\n\nLocation: 181 Main Road, Green Point\nWebsite: anewhotels.com/hotels/green-point/\n\n### Garden Court Victoria Junction\n\nThe chic Garden Court Victoria Junction Hotel is within walking distance of the V&A Waterfront and Two Oceans Aquarium. Amenities include access to the hotel’s outdoor pool, fitness centre, complimentary breakfast, business workspace, and free uncapped Wi-Fi.\n\nLocation: Corner of Somerset and Ebenezer Road, Greenpoint\nWebsite: bit.ly/4h1JNGH\n\n### La Splendida\n\nWith sweeping views of the Atlantic Ocean, La Splendida is your perfect seaside escape. Just steps downstairs, enjoy Mediterranean flavours and laid-back vibes at Sotano, a local favourite for tapas, coffee, and sundowners.\n\nLocation: 121 Beach Rd, Mouille Point\nWebsite: newmarkhotels.com\n\n## Related Articles\n\n- Pocket-friendly Sea Point\n- My Cape Town: Twin Venture’s Busi and Gugu\n\n## About Us\n\nCape Town Tourism is the city of Cape Town's official Regional Tourism Organisation, responsible for tourism marketing, visitor and industry services.\n\n## Recent Posts\n\nEat & Drink | Nightlife\n\n## 8 Best Streets for a Cape Town Night Out\n\nRead More\n\nBeach Activities | Nature Activities\n\n## How to Spend a Day at Cape Point\n\nRead More\n\nAnnouncements | Events | Information\n\n## Cape Town Creeps into the World’s Top 50 Halloween Destinations\n\nRead More\n\nSustainable Cape Town\n\n## Guardians of the Deep: Your Passport to Cape Town’s Coastline\n\nRead More\n\nInformation | Safety\n\n## Cape Town Tourism honours the frontline teams supporting visitors across the city\n\nRead More\n\nLimitless\n\n## 11 Wheelchair-Accessible Restaurants in Cape Town for Inclusive Dining\n\nRead More\n\nBeach Activities | Nature Activities | Outdoor Activities\n\n## Sunset Lovers, Here are the Best Sunset Spots in Cape Town\n\nRead More\n\nArts & Culture | Eat & Drink | Health & Wellness | Shopping\n\n## Cape Town Market Guide\n\nRead More\n\nNature Activities\n\n## Things to do in Cape Town as a Solo Traveller\n\nRead More\n\nBeach Activities | Eat & Drink | Information | Nature Activities\n\n## Free Things To Do In Cape Town\n\nRead More\n\n## Top Categories\n\n- Beach Activities\n- Shopping\n- Special Events\n- Nightlife\n- Nature Activities\n- Eat & Drink\n- Events\n- Health & Wellness\n- Information\n\n## Share with your friends!\n\n- Facebook\n- Twitter\n- Linkedin\n- Whatsapp\n\n#### Sign in\n\n- Sign in\n- Create account\n\nSign in\n\nLost your password?\n\n- Customer\n- Business\n\nCreate account\n\nYour account has been created. Please check your email for more details.\n\nPlease enter your email address. You will receive a link to create a new password via email.\n\nReset password\n\nPlease check your email for more details.\n\nSign in\n\n#### Send Message\n\n#### My favorites\n\nAccessibility\n\nAccessibility modes\n\nEpilepsy Safe Mode\n\nEpilepsy Safe Mode\n\nDampens color and removes blinks\n\nThis mode enables people with epilepsy to use the website safely by eliminating the risk of seizures that result from flashing or blinking animations and risky color combinations.\n\nVisually Impaired Mode\n\nVisually Impaired Mode\n\nImproves website's visuals\n\nThis mode adjusts the website for the convenience of users with visual impairments such as Degrading Eyesight, Tunnel Vision, Cataract, Glaucoma, and others.\n\nCognitive Disability Mode\n\nCognitive Disability Mode\n\nHelps to focus on specific content\n\nThis mode provides different assistive options to help users with cognitive impairments such as Dyslexia, Autism, CVA, and others, to focus on the essential elements of the website more easily.\n\nADHD Friendly Mode\n\nADHD Friendly Mode\n\nReduces distractions and improve focus\n\nThis mode helps users with ADHD and Neurodevelopmental disorders to read, browse, and focus on the main website elements more easily while significantly reducing distractions.\n\nBlindness Mode\n\nBlindness Mode\n\nAllows using the site with your screen-reader\n\nThis mode configures the website to be compatible with screen-readers such as JAWS, NVDA, VoiceOver, and TalkBack. A screen-reader is software for blind users that is installed on a computer and smartphone, and websites must be compatible with it.\n\nOnline Dictionary\n\nStart typing to search in Wikipedia\n\nReadable Experience\n\nContent Scaling\n\nDefault\n\nText Magnifier\n\nReadable Font\n\nDyslexia Friendly\n\nHighlight Titles\n\nHighlight Links\n\nFont Sizing\n\nDefault\n\nLine Height\n\nDefault\n\nLetter Spacing\n\nDefault\n\nLeft Aligned\n\nCenter Aligned\n\nRight Aligned\n\nVisually Pleasing Experience\n\nDark Contrast\n\nLight Contrast\n\nMonochrome\n\nHigh Contrast\n\nHigh Saturation\n\nLow Saturation\n\nAdjust Text Colors\n\nAdjust Title Colors\n\nAdjust Background Colors\n\nEasy Orientation\n\nMute Sounds\n\nHide Images\n\nHide Emoji\n\nReading Guide\n\nStop Animations\n\nReading Mask\n\nHighlight Hover\n\nHighlight Focus\n\nBig Dark Cursor\n\nBig Light Cursor\n\nCognitive Reading\n\nVirtual Keyboard\n\nNavigation Keys\n\nVoice Navigation\n\nLink navigator\n\nSelect an option\n\nReset Settings Hide Forever\n\nCape Town Tourism Accessibility Statement\n\n## Accessibility Statement\n\n- www.capetown.travel\n- 26 October 2026\n\n### Compliance status\n\nWe firmly believe that the internet should be available and accessible to anyone, and are committed to providing a website that is accessible to the widest possible audience, regardless of circumstance and ability.\n\nTo fulfill this, we aim to adhere as strictly as possible to the World Wide Web Consortium’s (W3C) Web Content Accessibility Guidelines 2.1 (WCAG 2.1) at the AA level. These guidelines explain how to make web content accessible to people with a wide array of disabilities. Complying with those guidelines helps us ensure that the website is accessible to all people: blind people, people with motor impairments, visual impairment, cognitive disabilities, and more.\n\nThis website utilizes various technologies that are meant to make it as accessible as possible at all times. We utilize an accessibility interface that allows persons with specific disabilities to adjust the website’s UI (user interface) and design it to their personal needs.\n\nAdditionally, the website utilizes an AI-based application that runs in the background and optimizes its accessibility level constantly. This application remediates the website’s HTML, adapts Its functionality and behavior for screen-readers used by the blind users, and for keyboard functions used by individuals with motor impairments.\n\nIf you’ve found a malfunction or have ideas for improvement, we’ll be happy to hear from you. You can reach out to the website’s operators by using the following email\n\n### Screen-reader and keyboard navigation\n\nOur website implements the ARIA attributes (Accessible Rich Internet Applications) technique, alongside various different behavioral changes, to ensure blind users visiting with screen-readers are able to read, comprehend, and enjoy the website’s functions. As soon as a user with a screen-reader enters your site, they immediately receive a prompt to enter the Screen-Reader Profile so they can browse and operate your site effectively. Here’s how our website covers some of the most important screen-reader requirements, alongside console screenshots of code examples:\n\n1. Screen-reader optimization: we run a background process that learns the website’s components from top to bottom, to ensure ongoing compliance even when updating the website. In this process, we provide screen-readers with meaningful data using the ARIA set of attributes. For example, we provide accurate form labels; descriptions for actionable icons (social media icons, search icons, cart icons, etc.); validation guidance for form inputs; element roles such as buttons, menus, modal dialogues (popups), and others. Additionally, the background process scans all the website’s images and provides an accurate and meaningful image-object-recognition-based description as an ALT (alternate text) tag for images that are not described. It will also extract texts that are embedded within the image, using an OCR (optical character recognition) technology. To turn on screen-reader adjustments at any time, users need only to press the Alt+1 keyboard combination. Screen-reader users also get automatic announcements to turn the Screen-reader mode on as soon as they enter the website. These adjustments are compatible with all popular screen readers, including JAWS and NVDA.\n2. Keyboard navigation optimization: The background process also adjusts the website’s HTML, and adds various behaviors using JavaScript code to make the website operable by the keyboard. This includes the ability to navigate the website using the Tab and Shift+Tab keys, operate dropdowns with the arrow keys, close them with Esc, trigger buttons and links using the Enter key, navigate between radio and checkbox elements using the arrow keys, and fill them in with the Spacebar or Enter key.Additionally, keyboard users will find quick-navigation and content-skip menus, available at any time by clicking Alt+1, or as the first elements of the site while navigating with the keyboard. The background process also handles triggered popups by moving the keyboard focus towards them as soon as they appear, and not allow the focus drift outside it. Users can also use shortcuts such as “M” (menus), “H” (headings), “F” (forms), “B” (buttons), and “G” (graphics) to jump to specific elements.\n\n### Disability profiles supported in our website\n\n- Epilepsy Safe Mode: this profile enables people with epilepsy to use the website safely by eliminating the risk of seizures that result from flashing or blinking animations and risky color combinations.\n- Visually Impaired Mode: this mode adjusts the website for the convenience of users with visual impairments such as Degrading Eyesight, Tunnel Vision, Cataract, Glaucoma, and others.\n- Cognitive Disability Mode: this mode provides different assistive options to help users with cognitive impairments such as Dyslexia, Autism, CVA, and others, to focus on the essential elements of the website more easily.\n- ADHD Friendly Mode: this mode helps users with ADHD and Neurodevelopmental disorders to read, browse, and focus on the main website elements more easily while significantly reducing distractions.\n- Blindness Mode: this mode configures the website to be compatible with screen-readers such as JAWS, NVDA, VoiceOver, and TalkBack. A screen-reader is software for blind users that is installed on a computer and smartphone, and websites must be compatible with it.\n- Keyboard Navigation Profile (Motor-Impaired): this profile enables motor-impaired persons to operate the website using the keyboard Tab, Shift+Tab, and the Enter keys. Users can also use shortcuts such as “M” (menus), “H” (headings), “F” (forms), “B” (buttons), and “G” (graphics) to jump to specific elements.\n\n### Additional UI, design, and readability adjustments\n\n1. Font adjustments – users, can increase and decrease its size, change its family (type), adjust the spacing, alignment, line height, and more.\n2. Color adjustments – users can select various color contrast profiles such as light, dark, inverted, and monochrome. Additionally, users can swap color schemes of titles, texts, and backgrounds, with over seven different coloring options.\n3. Animations – person with epilepsy can stop all running animations with the click of a button. Animations controlled by the interface include videos, GIFs, and CSS flashing transitions.\n4. Content highlighting – users can choose to emphasize important elements such as links and titles. They can also choose to highlight focused or hovered elements only.\n5. Audio muting – users with hearing devices may experience headaches or other issues due to automatic audio playing. This option lets users mute the entire website instantly.\n6. Cognitive disorders – we utilize a search engine that is linked to Wikipedia and Wiktionary, allowing people with cognitive disorders to decipher meanings of phrases, initials, slang, and others.\n7. Additional functions – we provide users the option to change cursor color and size, use a printing mode, enable a virtual keyboard, and many other functions.\n\n### Browser and assistive technology compatibility\n\nWe aim to support the widest array of browsers and assistive technologies as possible, so our users can choose the best fitting tools for them, with as few limitations as possible. Therefore, we have worked very hard to be able to support all major systems that comprise over 95% of the user market share including Google Chrome, Mozilla Firefox, Apple Safari, Opera and Microsoft Edge, JAWS and NVDA (screen readers).\n\n### Notes, comments, and feedback\n\nDespite our very best efforts to allow anybody to adjust the website to their needs. There may still be pages or sections that are not fully accessible, are in the process of becoming accessible, or are lacking an adequate technological solution to make them accessible. Still, we are continually improving our accessibility, adding, updating and improving its options and features, and developing and adopting new technologies. All this is meant to reach the optimal level of accessibility, following technological advancements. For any assistance, please reach out       "id": "https://www.capetownccid.org/sites/default/files/2023-08/State%20of%20Cape%20Town%20Central%20City%20Report%202022.pdf",
      "title": "STATE OF CAPE TOWN CENTRAL CITY REPORT",
      "url": "https://www.capetownccid.org/sites/default/files/2023-08/State%20of%20Cape%20Town%20Central%20City%20Report%202022.pdf",
      "text": "A YEAR IN REVIEW\n\nSTATE OF \nCAPE TOWN \nCENTRAL CITY \nREPORT \n2022 \n\nABOUT THE CAPE TOWN CENTRAL CITY\n\nThe Cape Town Central City is the traditional Central Business District (CBD) of the Cape Town metropole. For the purposes of this report, its geographical footprint is identical to that of the Cape Town Central City Improvement District (CCID), a not-for-profit private-public company that is mandated by property owners to provide services and promote this 1.6 km² Special Rates Area. This footprint is marked out by the broken yellow line on the map that appears on the inside front cover of this report, which is published by the CCID. All the information contained in this report is therefore only pertinent to this area. The area is bordered to the northeast by Table Bay Harbour (the Port of Cape Town), including the V&A Waterfront, and by the largely residential suburbs around the rest of the perimeter known as the Atlantic Seaboard (to the northwest), the City Bowl (to the west and south) and District Six and Woodstock (to the southeast).\n\nFor operational purposes, and for the purposes of this report, the area is divided into four precincts: Precinct 1 (known as the Foreshore, this is the medical, conferencing and financial precinct); Precinct 2 (known as the Inner City, this is the retail hub and heart of the CBD); Precinct 3 (the legal, leisure and cultural hub); and Precinct 4, referred to as the East City. All main road and rail transportation links in the Western Cape Province begin in the Cape Town CBD, including the N1 highway to the Gauteng province, and the N2 highway which travels along the southern coast of South Africa to the KwaZulu-Natal province and beyond. Cape Town International Airport lies on the N2, 19 km from the Central City. The CCID works closely with its partners, the City of Cape Town and the SA Police Service (SAPS), to maintain and ensure the Central City retains its reputation as the most economically successful and vibrant CBD in South Africa.\n\nCOVER IMAGE: ROBERT ANDERSON\n\nLEAVE THIS MAP OPEN AS YOU\n\nBROWSE THROUGH THIS REPORT\n\nThis publication has been designed so that readers can easily “find their way” around the\n\nCentral City, as the text often indicates in which of the four precincts that make up the\n\nCBD (P1 to P4) certain activities fall. These precincts fall within the operational boundaries of the Cape Town Central City Improvement\n\nDistrict (CCID). Opening the front cover entirely and having the map exposed while reading will enable quick referencing and orientation, and a better understanding of the economic activities in the different “regions”\n\nof our downtown, as contained in this report.\n\nPRECINCT 1 (THE FORESHORE)\n\nPRECINCT 2 (THE INNER CITY)\n\nPRECINCT 3 (LEGAL, LEISURE & CULTURAL HUB)\n\nPRECINCT 4 (THE EAST CITY)\n\n1\n\n2\n\n3\n\n4\n\nCTICC 1\n\nFounders\n\nGarden\n\nPier Place\n\nThibault Square\n\nCivic Centre\n\nArtscape\n\nCape Town Railway Station\n\nNorth Wharf Square\n\nWALTER SISULU AVE\n\nHERTZOG BOULEVARD\n\nL\n\nO\n\nW\n\nE\n\nR\n\nL\n\nO\n\nN\n\nG\n\nH\n\nE\n\nE\n\nR\n\nE\n\nN\n\nG\n\nR\n\nA\n\nC\n\nH\n\nT\n\nA\n\nD\n\nD\n\nE\n\nR\n\nL\n\nE\n\nY\n\nC\n\nH\n\nR\n\nIS\n\nT\n\nIA\n\nA\n\nN\n\nB\n\nA\n\nR\n\nN\n\nA\n\nR\n\nD\n\nL\n\nO\n\nO\n\nP\n\nB\n\nR\n\nE\n\nE\n\nOld Marine Drive\n\nHammerschlag\n\nJ a n\n\nD\n\nF\n\nM a la n\n\nMechau\n\nPrestwich\n\nRiebeek\n\nWaterkant\n\nB u r g\n\nHans Strijdom\n\nGreenmarket\n\nSquare\n\nSTRAND\n\nWALE\n\nL\n\nO\n\nN\n\nG\n\nB\n\nU\n\nI T\n\nE\n\nN\n\nG\n\nR\n\nA\n\nC\n\nH\n\nT\n\nSHORTMARKET\n\nHout\n\nLongmarket\n\nChurch\n\nCastle\n\nB u r g\n\nS t\n\nM a l l\n\nCity Hall\n\nGrand Parade\n\nHarrington\n\nSquare\n\nCommercial H\n\nBarrack\n\nAlbertus\n\nSpin C\n\nC\n\nA\n\nN\n\nT\n\nE\n\nR\n\nB\n\nU\n\nR\n\nY\n\nB\n\nU\n\nI T\n\nE\n\nN\n\nK\n\nA\n\nN\n\nT\n\nROELAND\n\nDARLING\n\nNELSON MANDELA BLVD\n\nCaledon\n\nT h e\n\nLeeuwen\n\nDorp\n\nBloem\n\nBuiten\n\nOrphan\n\nN e w\n\nA v e\n\nBUITENSINGEL\n\nO\n\nR\n\nAN\n\nG\n\nE\n\nPepper\n\nBloem\n\nANNANDALE\n\nH\n\nA\n\nT\n\nF\n\nIE\n\nL\n\nD\n\nP\n\nL\n\nE\n\nIN\n\nSIR LOWRY RD\n\nCastle of Good Hope\n\nSTRAND\n\nROELAND\n\nGreen\n\nD e a n\n\nOrphan Ln\n\nN1 TO GAUTENG\n\nTABLE BAY HARBOUR\n\nTO N2\n\nVasco Da Gama\n\nCTICC 2\n\nThe Western Cape and Cape Town are back! After a gruelling couple of years under the spectre of Covid-19, our economy has clawed its way back, and this is in large part thanks to our citizens and the private sector. Their grit, tenacity, resourcefulness, and resilience shone through this difficult time, ensuring our province and the Mother City endured. But we are now faced with an even bigger challenge. The energy crisis has emerged as the most significant threat to our recovery and potential. But as the Western Cape Government, we cannot simply look elsewhere to others for solutions while the situation worsens, leaving livelihoods and development in the dark, dragging down all we have achieved since the pandemic. We have stepped up and stepped in to intervene, ploughing vast resources into mitigating the impact of ceaseless power cuts to\n\nkeep crucial services running. As a government, we have allocated more than R1 billion to short-, medium- and long-term solutions. If you add what municipalities in our province are also budgeting to battle the power crisis, it amounts to nearly R7 billion over the next couple of years to protect water, sanitation, healthcare, and telecommunication services, among other services, as much as we can. Through these efforts – together with our mission to keep the Western Cape moving in the right direction through our encouraging track record of creating more jobs and enabling businesses, and of offering dignity and hope to our citizens when they need it the most – we will continue to work closely with our partners like the CCID, which is doing a phenomenal job in promoting Cape Town, and its Central City, as the investment destination of choice in Africa and the world.\n\nI welcome the publication of this, the 11 th annual edition of the CCID’s State of Cape Town Central City Report 2022 – A year in review. For the past decade, this report has offered a wonderfully detailed review of the trends in the Cape Town CBD and has become vital reading for anyone with an interest in Cape Town’s business and property space. Fortunately, there is much good news and progress to report. While our country is facing a range of serious challenges – from the energy crisis and widespread infrastructure collapse to a stalled economy and dangerously high unemployment – Cape Town is increasingly bucking all those trends. There are several reasons for this, but one of the most significant is the fact that we have a vibrant, attractive and safe city centre, and we’re the only metro in South Africa that can honestly make that claim.\n\nMuch of the credit for this must go the tireless work done by the CCID in its undertaking to manage and position Cape Town’s Central City as a premier investment destination and business hub. Since taking office in 2021, I have really come to appreciate the power of partnerships. In Cape Town, we welcome and embrace the massive contributions that NGOs, the business sector and community organisations make to critical government functions such as crime fighting, housing delivery and social assistance. In the CCID, we have one of our most valuable partners, and the speed at which our city centre managed to bounce back after two years of Covid-19 disruptions is testament to the contribution this organisation makes to Cape Town’s economic health. I look forward to reading this highly regarded report from cover to cover.\n\nALAN WINDE Premier of the Western Cape\n\nGEORDIN HILL-LEWIS Executive Mayor of Cape Town\n\nCONTENTS\n\n2 Letter from the CEO \n& chairperson of the \nCCID Board \n4 Year in review \n6 The future of the \nCentral Business \nDistrict \n8 Cape Town in context \n10 A business-friendly \ncity \n12 Investing in the \nMother City \n14 Investment partners \n16 The Central City in \nnumbers \n18 Doing business in \nthe Central City \n19 Breakdown of \nbusinesses in \nthe Central City \n\nSECTION 1: CAPE TOWN POST-COVID\n\nSECTION 2: OPEN FOR BUSINESS\n\n20 Property investment \nmap \n22 Aerial view of property \ninvestments 2022 \n24 Commercial property \ntrends \n28 Office vacancy rates \n30 Parking in the Central \nCity \n32 Residential property \ntrends \n34 Residential values \n& rentals \n38 Residential property \nmap \n40 10 years of living in \ntown \n\nSECTION 3: PROPERTY FILE\n\n42 Overview \n43 State of Central City \nretail in 2022 \n44 Retail economy \nin figures \n46 Retail confidence \nsurvey \n48 Retail occupancy rates \n49 Retail occupancy \nsummary \n50 Visitor economy \nreport \n52 Ships ahoy: port \noverview \n53 Just cruising \n54 Central City event \nvenues \n\nSECTION 4: CENTRAL CITY ECONOMIES\n\n56 Precinct 1: \nThe Foreshore \n62 Precinct 2: \nThe inner city \n68 Precinct 3: \nLegal, leisure \n& cultural hub \n74 Precinct 4: \nThe East City \n\nSECTION 5: CENTRAL CITY PRECINCTS\n\n80 IN CONCLUSION\n\nCAPE TOWN 1POST-COVID\n\nSECTION\n\nLETTER FROM THE CEO & CHAIRPERSON OF THE BOARD\n\nThere’s no doubt that the past two years have been tough for Central Business Districts (CBDs) globally. In Cape Town, the economic challenges we faced in our CBD were real. We are therefore very pleased to report in this 11 th edition of the Cape Town Central City Improvement District’s State of Cape Town Central\n\nCity Report 2022 – A year in review that we have turned a corner and put the pandemic behind us. Twenty twenty two ushered in a new dawn for our Central City, and by mid-year – when all Covid-19 restrictions had been lifted – our economy stabilised and experienced growth, with the number of businesses increasing from 2 981\n\nin 2021 to 3 116 in 2022. The retail sector, which dominates the Central City economy, also grew from 1 163 entities in 2021 to 1 243 in 2022. Behind these statistics are resilient, resourceful entrepreneurs who kept the faith, read the changing market, and made strategic business decisions to keep their doors open.\n\nSECTION 1\n\n3 116 THE NUMBER OF BUSINESSES IN OUR DOWNTOWN INCREASED FROM 2 981 IN 2021 TO 3 116 IN 2022.\n\nNorth Wharf Square\n\nThe Rubik\n\nCommercial landlords, in a successful bid to slow down and ultimately reverse the Work From Home trend (which steadily lost ground last year), continued to encourage tenants to return to the workplace by creating more tactile and interactive office environments. With the construction sector regaining it’s pre-Covid-19 momentum, the CBD’s skyline was punctuated by scores of yellow cranes hovering over building sites, and we are pleased to report that the total value of property developments in the CBD, conservatively estimated, stood at R3.555 billion last year. With 22 developments or redevelopments on the cards (including residential, commercial and mixed-use builds amongst others), the majority of these were residential, indicating a strong demand for inner-city living.\n\nThree of the stand-out builds under construction were One Thibault (R500 million), The Rubik (R600 million) and 84 Harrington (R180 million), the world’s tallest building to be constructed from hempcrete blocks. The stellar residential build under construction was The Fynbos (R1.200 billion) – destined to become Africa’s first biophilic building. With the overall value of all CBD property set at R42.9 billion (according to the City of Cape Town’s 2022 property evaluation), these new builds added to the ongoing dynamism of our CBD in 2022 as it opened its doors to more and more digital nomads and solo travellers, and also continued to reap the rewards of the ongoing “semigration” of South Africans to the Western Cape province.\n\nKey to our success as the country’s most economically sound CBD was also our renewed collaboration with the City of Cape Town. We are grateful to Executive Mayor Geordin Hill-Lewis and his team for this partnership and for working with us to ensure that Cape Town, and its CBD, remains a business-friendly city.\n\nTASSO \nEVANGELINOS \nCEO: Cape Town \nCCID \n\nROB KANE Chairperson: Cape Town CCID + CEO: Boxwood Property Fund\n\nSECTION 1\n\nYEAR IN REVIEWWith the World Bank warning of a possible global recession due to the “rapid deterioration of growth prospects coupled with rising inflation and tightening of financial conditions”, 2022 presented a plethora of economic pressures. Here is a brief overview of last year.\n\nJust as economic upheaveal generated by the global pandemic began to subside and life was beginning to return to normal, the Russian invasion of Ukraine sent shockwaves through the global economy. Initially it was thought that the price pressures triggered by the pandemic would be transitory but the renewed disruption to global supply chains from the war – particularly in the food and energy markets – further entrenched the price pressures. This prompted central banks around the world to hike interest rates aggressively, reversing the supportive environment created during the pandemic. Locally, the prime rate rose by 325 basis points during 2022, ending the year at 10.5 % – slightly higher than the peak just prior to the onset of Covid-19. Real GDP growth dropped to 2.0 % in 2022 from 4.9 % in 2021, mainly brought about by power cuts by State-owned Eskom, constraints in the transport sector, floods in KwaZulu Natal and the global disruption due to the Russia-Ukraine war.\n\nINFLATION RISES \nInflation rose to 6.9 % in 2022 from \n4.5 % in 2021, driven by higher food \nand fuel prices. To curb rising inflation, \nthe Reserve Bank of South Africa raised \nthe prime rate from 7.5 % in January \n2022 to 10.5 % by year-end 1. \nThe rand depreciated from 15.3 \nper USD in January 2022 to 17.3 \nin December 2022. \n\nCOVID BANS ARE LIFTED After more than two years of disruption and uncertainty caused by the pandemic, one of the key milestones in 2022 was the lifting of the National State of Disaster in April 2022 – with only “transitionary measures” such as the wearing of masks, limits on the size of gatherings and international travel remaining in force for a further 30 days. These transitionary measures were repealed in June 2022. The lifting of the final Covid-19 restrictions resulted in a marked improvement in confidence around the country, and among the CBD’s retailers – as measured by the CCID’s quarterly Retail Confidence Survey.\n\nTOURISM RECOVERS Both local and international tourism, which were both hard-hit by the pandemic, rebounded in 2022. A total of 5.8 million foreign tourists 2 visited South Africa last year – a massive 152.6 % increase from 2021 – although not matching the 10 million visitors recorded in 2019. According to the Western Cape Government, Cape Town International Airport recorded a robust recovery in two-way passenger numbers during the peak season month of December compared to pre-pandemic numbers. International passenger numbers in December 2022 showed a 95 % recovery\n\ncompared to December 2019 while domestic two-way passengers recorded a 71 % recovery.\n\nSEMIGRANTS FLOCK TO THE WESTERN CAPE The pandemic triggered a wave of relocations within South Africa, as remote working became a reality for many employees. The growing reputation of the Western Cape and Cape Town in particular for reliable municipal services encouraged a net influx of residents and businesses to the city. This contributed to the improved office vacancy rate last year and fuelled the ongoing conversion of office space to residential units within the Central City.\n\nPOWER CUTS FUEL SOLAR BOOM During 2022, Eskom was unable to match the country’s demand for electricity, resulting in a total of 3 775 hours – or more than 200 days – of power cuts (known locally as loadshedding). While this disrupted economic activity and weighed on business and consumer confidence, it also triggered a solar investment boom. It is estimated that in 2022, the country imported solar panels worth more than R5 billion – up from R4 billion in 2021. These are expected to provide sufficient generating capacity in 2023 to reduce loadshedding by an additional stage. 3\n\n1 https://www.afdb.org/en/countries/southern-africa/south-africa/south-africa-economic-outlook \n2 https://www.gov.za/speeches/ \n3 PWC SA Economic Outlook, January 2023 \n\nAs cities face an inflection point three years after the start of the pandemic, Central Business Districts – traditionally the commercial heart of a metropolitan area – will have to reinvent themselves to remain attractive and competitive urban centres.\n\nTHE FUTUREOF THE CENTRAL BUSINESS DISTRICT\n\nWhat does a successful CBD look like in the 2030s? According to a JLL Global Research report on trends in global cities (from Hong Kong, Tokyo and Singapore to Paris, Amsterdam, Sydney, Dallas, Los Angeles and Vancouver), to survive and thrive in the years that lie ahead, CBDs need to transform “and that will only be achieved through an ecosystem of policymakers, local authorities, investors, developers and major tenants working together”. In response to changes in how people use CBDs, how they interact with the built environment, the public and private sectors will need to implement best practices in terms of infrastructure and sustainability. The thinking is that CBDs will move from primarily being places of work to becoming mixed-use destinations that are able to capitalise on being at the heart of transport networks “and complemented by an array of educational, cultural and government institutions”.\n\nSHIFT IN URBAN DYNAMICS Significant change is imminent in urban hubs throughout the world following the pandemic which prompted a shift in how people live and work. This shift in urban dynamics is being experienced in CBDs which are bearing the brunt of subdued demand for office space, changing commuter patterns and flows due to the Work From Home trend and a desire for “experience based” spaces. CBDs therefore face a fundamental need to reinvent themselves to attract visitors, investors, residents and workers.\n\nINNOVATIVE TECHNOLOGIES Creating a CBD that works for everybody is key to its success. The process, the report says, involves discovering and implementing innovative technologies and policies that will improve the quality of life of people and the built environment’s contribution to solving environmental, demographic and economic issues. Key to unlocking the potential of CBDs is finding other uses for existing properties. “Perhaps the most visible of these is repurposing uncompetitive offices to residential, hotel, cultural or other non-work functions.” This is not to say that all commercial buildings which have fallen out of favour post-Covid need to be converted to non-office use. Thoughtful office refurbishments have been rewarded with 10,7 % rental growth since the onset of the pandemic in the CBDs of some international cities. This has proved the resilience\n\nof traditional CBD locations and indicates there is a supply gap in the market for “opportunistic and proactive investment”.\n\nAGEING REAL ESTATE The amount of ageing real estate in CBDs is cause for concern, presenting a major challenge for both occupancy and capital value preservation. With tenants showing a preference for newer space, buildings, especially historical buildings, that have been extensively and sensitively refurbished are in demand. This strategy will be particularly useful in cities pursuing “renovation-first” policies. Refurbishing buildings also comes at a lower environmental cost than new developments: the carbon impact of less than 500 kg of CO2 per m² is far below the 500-1 500 kg seen in new builds.\n\nTHREATS A challenge for CBDs has been the growth of, and migration to, destination precincts or “submarkets” on the urban fringe which have shifted investor focus from more established locations. “Emerging districts such as Fulton Market in Chicago, Shoreditch and King’s Cross in London, MediaSpree in Berlin and Roppongi in Tokyo are providing a framework for traditional CBDs\n\nSECTION 1\n\nTo successfully adapt to the future, CBDs will require proactive engagement from owners, developers and authorities.\n\nof a much longer recovery for business travel compared with leisure travel.\n\nLONG-TERM PROSPECTS CBDs can be transformed over the long term if they successfully evolve into multi-purpose destinations, with mixed-use buildings and sustainability-oriented design. Interventions, such as breaking up “superblocks” and creating pleasing pedestrian areas with attractive retail offerings and a variety of businesses, will be critical to making CBDs more attractive.\n\nto emulate through building upon the traits that make these neighbourhoods so successful in attracting occupiers and investors.” Other challenges include new hybrid and remote-working arrangements, competition with other precincts, long commutes, and a lack of consistent footfall.\n\nUNSTABLE DEMAND Hospitality and retail are also at the mercy of changes to demand for CBD property as their revenues are sensitive to travel trends and restrictions. Asia Pacific remains most challenged by\n\nrestrictive travel policies since the onset of the pandemic while the Middle East and Africa have seen a particularly strong performance. Globally, CBDs will have to address sustained volatility and traveller preferences, including the possibility\n\nSOURCE: JLL Global Research: The Future of the Central Business District – Creating dynamic urban centers. May 2023\n\nSituated in the Western Cape province of South Africa, the city of Cape Town boasts the most successful Central Business District (CBD) in the country. This CBD, also known as the Central City or “town” to locals, drives the economy of the greater Cape metropole. The information on these pages provides potential investors with economic insight into this area.\n\nGROSS DOMESTIC PRODUCT (GDP) 1\n\nLatest available data (EPIC Q3 2022) South Africa: R4.6 trillion Western Cape: R660 billion Cape Town: typically contributes 71 % of the provincial GDP annually. GROSS VALUE-ADDED SECTORS Latest available data (EPIC Q3 2022) In 2021, the city’s highest gross\n\nvalue-added (GVA) sectors in its \neconomy were: \nFinance: +35.7 % \nCommunity services: +20.4 % \nTrade: +14.9 % \nManufacturing: +13.8 % \nTransport: +8.6 % \nGDP PER CAPITA 2 \n\nLatest available data (EPIC Q3 2022) South Africa: R103 198 Western Cape: R123 709 Cape Town: R135 580\n\nAIR TRAVEL 3 During 2022, 7 876 183 people passed through Cape Town International Airport. The Western Cape Government reports that preliminary statistics from Airports Company South Africa show that Cape Town International Airport recorded 266 656 international two-way passenger numbers in December 2022 – a 95 % recovery compared to December 2019. During the same month, the domestic\n\nOPEN FOR 2BUSINESS\n\nSECTION\n\nCAPE TOWN IN CONTEXT\n\nAfrica’s Leading City Destination for the 2 nd consecutive year (World Travel Awards) Most Sustainable City in Africa according to the Corporate Knights Sustainable Cities Index\n\nNinth Best City in the World according to Condé Nast Traveller Table Mountain named Africa’s Leading Tourist Attraction for the 4 th consecutive year (World Travel Awards)\n\nMost Promising Muslim-friendly City Destination (Halal Travel Awards) Third Best Global City according to The Telegraph\n\nCAPE TOWN ACCOLADES\n\n2022\n\n7 876 183 During 2022, 7 876 183 people passed through Cape Town International Airport.\n\nterminal recorded a 71 % rebound in two-way passenger numbers (790 514) compared to the 2019 figure.\n\nESTIMATED POPULATION RATES (2022) 4\n\nSouth Africa: 60 142 978 Western Cape: 7 113 776 (11.8 % of national population) Cape Town: 4 678 900\n\nEMPLOYMENT The number of people employed in Cape Town increased by 80 820 individuals from year-earlier levels in Q3 2022 (latest available data), bringing the total number of people employed to 1.54 million. Six sectors have shown signs of sustained recovery, while four sectors continued to shed jobs compared to the same period in 2021. Trade, hotels & restaurants created the most jobs (+61 878) in Q3, followed by community, social & other personal services (+40 062) and manufacturing (+27 381). Construction registered the largest job losses during Q3 (-21 571 jobs) compared to levels recorded earlier in the year. In Q3 2022, Cape Town had the second lowest strict unemployment rate at 26.8 % but the lowest broad unemployment rate 5 at 29.5 % compared to other metros.\n\nEleventh in Time Out’s Best Cities in the World to Visit in 2022 Cape Town International Airport named Africa’s Leading Airport for the 6 th consecutive year (World Travel Awards)\n\nThe Silo Hotel named \nAfrica’s Leading \nLuxury Hotel \n(World Travel Awards) \nFYN Restaurant ranked \n37 in World’s 50 Best \nRestaurants and \nBest in Africa \n\nPepperclub Hotel voted Continent Winner in the Luxury City Hotel category of the 16 th World Luxury Hotel Awards\n\n1 At constant 2015 prices, seasonally adjusted \nand annualised. \n2 At current prices \n3 Airports Company South Africa \n4 EPIC Q3 2022 \n5 Broad unemployment rate includes unemployed \npeople not actively seeking employment. \n\nA BUSINESS\n\nFRIENDLY CITY Cape Town is on a mission to become the easiest place to do business in Africa with the City introducing an Ease-of-Doing-Business Index to track its progress.\n\nBusinesses play a crucial role in Cape Town’s prosperity, and their success is vital for its future growth and sustainability especially as the city’s economy recovers from the impact of the pandemic. In a bid to create conditions to allow businesses to flourish, the City of Cape Town has developed an ease of-doing-business programme with the aim of creating not only the most business-friendly environment in the country but on the continent. This attempt by the City to make it easier for companies and people to do business with its administration began in 2016 after a Subnational Doing Business Report, published by the World Bank and National Treasury, revealed that South African entrepreneurs faced significant regulatory challenges depending on their business’s location. Cape Town was not conducive to starting and running a business. Based on these findings, the City came up with a plan to enhance service delivery. Now it has ramped up its efforts to improve its business processes with the vision of becoming Africa’s most business-friendly city. Some of its goals include: Ensuring a culture of business friendliness throughout the organisation Leveraging the latest technology to improve service delivery Establishing public and private growth partnerships in priority industries Ensuring the delivery of quality services that are efficient, reliable, cost-effective and timely Ensuring that a sustainable supply of well-positioned commercial and industrial land is available to develop a quality built environment that meets the needs of businesses Ensuring regulations align with business interests\n\nEASE-OF-DOING-BUSINESS \nINDEX \nA central part of the programme, the \nCity’s progress will be tracked by an \nEase-of-Doing-Business Index. This \nwill provide business owners and \ninvestors with a clear and concise \nview of the City’s journey. \nWith a focus on Urban Mobility; \nSafety & Security; Film & Events; \nWater; Economic Growth; Finance; \nEnergy; Property Planning, \nDevelopment & Management and \nIS&T, the Ease-of-Doing-Business \nindicators are: \nTransferring public land \nGetting land-use rights \nGetting building-plan approval \nWayleaves \nGetting electricity \nConnecting to the water network \nGetting a revenue (rates) clearance \ncertificate \nObtaining a business licence \nInformal trading permits \nDigitisation \nIn a year’s time, the City hopes to \nshow a marked improvement on its \njourney to becoming Africa’s easiest \nplace to do business. \n\nSOURCE: Ease of doing business in Cape Town (Invest Cape Town/City of Cape Town)\n\nPuma Select\n\nThe Rockefeller\n\nCookie Co.\n\nSECTION 2\n\n‘A thriving and successful business community is vital to achieve our vision of Cape Town being a city of hope for all’ – Geordin Hill-Lewis\n\nINVESTING IN THE\n\nMOTHER CITYThe city of Cape Town consistently ranks as South Africa’s top investment destination. Here’s why.\n\nLOCATION Cape Town is situated in the Western Cape, home to some of the country’s most beautiful scenery. The Western Cape has an abundance of natural assets, including world-renowned coastlines, the iconic Table Mountain National Park and Kirstenbosch National Botanical Garden, along with an abundance of orchards and vineyards. The province is also one of the most successful in the country, with established industries including tourism and agriculture, and it has an inspiring industrial hub. This has encouraged a shift of certain economic activities to the Cape, enticing foreign firms and new investment groups to invest in the local scene, and prompted a “semigration” trend with other South Africans moving to Western Cape post-Covid. Four top universities, including the University of Cape Town and Stellenbosch University, and two globally recognised business schools in the region continue to attract innovation and talent. These institutions have boosted Cape Town’s status as the digitech hub of Africa and a leader in African BPO (business process outsourcing) innovation. Fields like business process management have been leading creators of jobs in Cape Town over the past few years.\n\nACCESSIBILITY \nAir Access \nCape Town International Airport \nis Africa’s third largest airport. It \nis considered to be the best on the \ncontinent, having won the Skytrax \nWorld Airport Awards “Best Airport \n\nin Africa” award at least six times. Located 20 km from the Central City, the airport is integrated with other city transport infrastructures for ease of travel. The R7 billion upgrading and expansion of CTIA – involving the refurbishment of the domestic arrivals terminal, the expansion and upgrade of the international terminal and the construction of a new realigned runway worth R3.93 billion – will allow CTIA to accommodate larger aircraft and increase its per-hour landings and departures. Cape Town Air Access, a global air-route development agency within Wesgro, reports that the Western Cape aviation sector experienced\n\na positive recovery in 2022, with continued growth expected in 2023. During 2022, the domestic terminal processed 5.96 million two-way passengers, with domestic terminal two-way passenger recovery at 71 % compared to 2019. The international terminal saw a recovery of 73 % in 2022, compared with the same period in 2019, with almost 1.9 million two-way passengers passing through the terminal, Air Access reports. The terminal’s average load factor reached pre-Covid levels in 2022, recording 72 %. Passenger performance at the international terminal continues to grow, with a record recovery of 97 % in January 2023, compared to 2019, with over\n\nSECTION 2\n\n280 000 two-way passengers passing \nthrough the terminal. \nTwo-way cargo volumes in the \nWestern Cape reached over 47 000 \nmetric tonnes, representing a \n75 % recovery compared to the \nsame period in 2019. \nPort Access \nThe Port of Cape Town, the \nsecond busiest in South Africa, is \nstrategically positioned and serves \ncargoes (especially containers) \nmoving between Europe or the \nAmericas and the Middle East \nor Australia. \nExpansion is planned for the \nContainer Terminal to allow it to \naccommodate larger vessels. \nThe port also facilitates a growing \ncruise economy, which bounced \nback after Covid-19 with the \n2022/2023 season welcoming \n145 000 cruise passengers and \n42 000 crew members. A total of \n75 ships (including five new ships) \nwith 41 turnaround visits sailed into \nthe port. This is almost double the \nnumber of ship calls when compared \nto the last complete cruise season in \n2019/2020, when there were \n39 ship visits with an estimated \n42 000 arriving passengers. \nRoad Access \nCape Town and the Western Cape \nare linked to the other provinces \nof South Africa by two major road \nnetworks, the N1 and the N2. \n\nHUB OF OPPORTUNITY Home to the leading convention centre in Africa, the Cape Town International Convention Centre, the Mother City is shifting to a service-driven economy with an established business culture and a cluster of trusted financial institutions. There is confidence in the soundness of banks in the region, rated third in the world by the World Economic Forum.\n\nThe City of Cape Town received a clean audit award for the 2021/2022 financial year. The city was lauded by Auditor-general Tsakani Maluleke for implementing a proactive system of accountability, with a “zero-tolerance culture towards noncompliance”.\n\nECONOMY & INFRASTRUCTURE Cape Town is a sub-Saharan African business hub with a sophisticated economy, and is an ideal destination for industry and innovation. The city has everything in place for sustainable economic growth – from its world-class Central City to its established infrastructure across several sectors. Cape Town’s service-driven economy has grown at a higher rate than the national average thanks to its competitive advantage in key industries and sectors, including several manufacturing sub-sectors such as electronic and electrical products, metal, steel and beverages. Compared with other international cities, it offers excellent value for investors. The city has a steadily growing fintech sector; rapidly expanding B2B and B2C e-commerce sectors; increased exports; and an improved trade balance. With its expansive agricultural surroundings, Cape Town acts as a processing, trade and retail hub for a wide range of export-quality produce. Cape Town hosts a globally recognised film and media production industry.\n\nENABLING BUSINESS ENVIRONMENT As a global mid-sized developing city, Cape Town actively facilitates the creation of an enabling business environment in which entrepreneurs can flourish. Sectors poised for expansion are trade, manufacturing, information technology, telecommunications, medical and research equipment and other high tech processes. The City’s strategic and targeted approach aims to create the right conditions for growth. This includes reliable infrastructure and providing relief from Eskom power cuts (known locally as loadshedding). Cape Town is also building a sound “green” reputation. Several new initiatives and the founding of several renewable company head offices in the city are leading the renewable energy and clean-tech industries.\n\nLOW UNEMPLOYMENT The city’s unemployment rate remains stable. In Q3 of 2022, the city had the second lowest strict unemployment rate at 26.8 % but the lowest broad unemployment rate at 29.5 % compared to other metros.\n\nRESILIENCE STRATEGY Cape Town’s Resilience Strategy, developed after the city emerged in 2018 from the worst drought in recorded history, offers a roadmap for a 21st century metropolis. Exposed to extreme weather, and subject to extreme spatial divides created during the Apartheid era, the city is vulnerable to increased heat and decreased rainfall. To enable it to survive chronic stresses and acute shocks, it has developed a resilience strategy to allow it to adapt and thrive. With respect to power cuts, the City of Cape Town is on track with plans to protect residents within three years from the first four stages of power cuts implemented by State-owned utility, Eskom. The plan involves three stages of procurement. Executive Mayor Geordin Hill-Lewis says this is vital for economic growth and investor confidence.\n\nSOURCES: Invest Cape Town, Wesgro, City of Cape Town EPIC 2020 Quarter 3, Statistics South Africa\n\nCAPE TOWN CENTRAL CITY IMPROVEMENT DISTRICT (CCID) For 22 years, the CCID has pulled out all the stops to ensure the Cape Town CBD is a safe and clean urban environment that is investment friendly. During the dark days of the pandemic, the CCID maintained a consistent presence in the Central City, ensuring it remained a welcoming destination and a working and resilient inner city. A map delineating its geographical boundaries and the four precincts that fall within it can be found inside the front cover of this publication.\n\nKey partnerships across the private and public sector facilitate the fostering of a stable economy and promote business and property investment in the Cape Town Central City.\n\nINVESTMENT\n\nPARTNERS\n\nThe CCID, like the other 51 city improvement districts in the Cape metropole, is a non-profit company that exists in terms of the City of Cape Town’s Municipal Property Rates Act, Section 22 [Special Rates Area (SRA)] and the SRA bylaw. It provides complementary top-up services within a specific geographical area, to support the primary agencies, namely the City of Cape Town and the South African Police Service (SAPS). A public-private partnership which is overseen by a board of directors, the CCID has three operational departments – Safety & Security,\n\nUrban Management and Social Development. Its Communications department collaborates across these three to promote the CCID’s work and business and property investment into the Central City. A fifth department manages financial and HR-related administration. With a full-time staff of 20 people, the CCID oversees a total workforce of around 600 people who carry out its mandate to manage the public spaces between the buildings of Cape Town’s traditional downtown area. WHERE: One Thibault, cnr Long St & Hans Strijdom Ave (Precinct 1) www.capetownccid.org\n\nFor 22 years, the CCID has pulled out all the stops to ensure the Cape Town CBD is a safe and clean urban environment that is investment friendly.\n\nHeerengracht\n\nWESGRO As the official tourism, investment and trade promotion agency for Cape Town and the Western Cape, Wesgro aims to attract and retain foreign direct investment, grow exports and market Cape Town and the province as a competitive business and leisure destination globally. The agency promotes economic activity in the province to facilitate job creation by landing and keeping businesses in the Western Cape and helping local businesses to export beyond South Africa’s borders. It looks to align the region to national priorities for economic growth, trade and investment promotion initiatives and facilitates the link between business and government decision-makers. It is often the first port of call for foreign buyers, local exporters and investors looking to take advantage of the region’s potential. WHERE: SA Reserve Bank Building, 60 St Georges Mall (Precinct 2) www.wesgro.co.za\n\nTHE WESTERN CAPE ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENT PARTNERSHIP (EDP) The EDP is a non-profit company established in 2012 as a collaborative intermediary organisation to work with and between stakeholder sectors in the Western Cape economic delivery system. It aims to improve the performance of the Cape Town and Western Cape economic development system, by creating and sustaining partnerships between economic stakeholders, in support of the goals of creating a resilient, inclusive and competitive region. Funded by national, provincial and municipal government, the EDP has played a unique role in bringing together the public and private sectors, academia and civil society, to focus on specific issues identified as key drivers of economic growth. It focuses on solutions that improve the local and regional economic system.\n\nWHERE: Tannery Office Park, Belmont Road, Rondebosch www.wcedp.co.za\n\nCAPE CHAMBER OF COMMERCE & INDUSTRY Representing businesses of all sizes in virtually all sectors, the Cape Chamber of Commerce & Industry is mandated to serve, enable and lead business. This is achieved via a multitude of services and networking opportunities as well as robust advocacy on behalf of business. An institution in the Western Cape, it was established in 1804. WHERE: 33 Martin Hammerschlag Way, Foreshore (Precinct 1) www.capechamber.co.za\n\nINVEST CAPE TOWN Invest Cape Town promotes investment and funding, attracts talent to the city and helps companies and entrepreneurs discover new opportunities in Cape Town. It works alongside Wesgro to facilitate investment and has a business enquiry service. Launched in 2016 by the City of Cape Town, it builds the city’s brand as a world-class investment destination. The Investor Centre offices of the initiative opened in 2017, at the same time as those of InvestSA Western Cape, to function as a collaborative one-stop shop for potential investors in Cape Town and the Western Cape province. WHERE: Media City Building, 1 Heerengracht, Foreshore (Precinct 1) www.investcapetown.com\n\nINVESTSA WESTERN CAPE The national Department of Trade & Industry (DTI) established InvestSA offices in major South African centres. The Western Cape InvestSA One Stop Shop (InvestSA OSS) opened in the Central City in 2017 to promote investment into the province by streamlining regulatory procedures and providing investors with services to fast-track projects and reduce government red tape when starting a business. InvestSA Western Cape OSS focuses on the coordination and incorporation of the special economic zones, provincial investment agencies, local authorities and the relevant government departments involved in regulatory, registration, permits and licencing matters. Wesgro is the management entity. WHERE: 46 St Georges Mall (Precinct 2) www.investsa.gov.za\n\nACCELERATE CAPE TOWN Established in 2006, Accelerate Cape Town represents top-tier Cape Town businesses. It provides a collaborative platform to facilitate business growth in the province, in partnership with corporate Cape Town, tertiary institutions and regional government. It strives to promote business unity, advocates for policies and practices that support economic growth, and conceptualises and implements collaborative projects. WHERE: The Ridge, 6 Marina Road, Portswood District, V&A Waterfront www.acceleratecapetown.co.za\n\nPier Place\n\nTHE CENTRAL CITY\n\nIN NUMBERS BUSINESSES IN THE CBD There are 3 116 entities doing business in the Cape Town Central City. 2022 13.3 %\n\nTotal retail space in the Central City 271 209 m²\n\nRetail vacancy rate as at Q4 2022 4.5 %\n\n29 335 Number of people using government facilities daily\n\nNON-RETAIL ENTITIES\n\nCape Town’s Central City economy held its own post-Covid-19, with retail showing impressive growth in spite of consumers increasingly facing economic hardship. A total of 80 new retailers opened in the Central City in 2022. The number of restaurants increased from 145 in 2021 to 147 in 2022 (excluding coffee shops and takeaways), and included the opening of destination eateries including Ramenhead, a casual Japanese eatery from the award-winning FYN stable. In 2022, lockdown levels eased and eventually stopped mid-year, allowing the local economy, especially the night-time economy, opportunity to recover and grow, with several sectors recording a positive output. This was also reflected in the Central City economy: 10 of the 17 economic sectors operating in the CBD grew in\n\nEvery year trends, patterns and shifts in the way business is being conducted in the Central City emerge. Here’s a succinct analysis of what went down in 2022.\n\n## DOING BUSINESS\n\n### IN THE CENTRAL CITY\n\nPier Place Portside\n\nSECTION 2\n\nsize, with the number of business entities overall increasing by 135 from 2 981 to 3 116. Retail, the largest sector, once again increased its footprint by the biggest margin, with 1 243 business operating in the CBD in 2022 as opposed to 1 163 in 2021. The second-largest sector – legal services – once again experienced the second-largest growth, with the number of entities recorded in 2021 (655) increasing to 681 in 2022. This bodes well for the return of office workers to the CBD as does the fact that nine new general corporates & head offices made the CBD their base in 2022. Eighteen new medical practices also opened their doors. Only five of the 17 sectors experienced negative growth: these included the education and resources sector (from 76 to 70); artistic studios (from 29 to 24); and finance, investment, insurance & banking (from 175 to 171). These figures reveal a resilient retail sector which was able to absorb the shocks of Covid-19.\n\n3 116 In 2022, the number of businesses in the Central City increased from 2981 to 3 116.\n\nBREAKDOWN OF BUSINESSES IN THE CENTRAL CITY A total of 3 116 businesses were operating in the Central City at the end of 2022, an increase of 135 from 2021 and 205 less than 2019, just before the pandemic.
